# Construction Bid Prices vs Engineer's Estimates: 2017 to 2024 Trends

<script type="application/ld+json">
{
  "@context": "https://schema.org",
  "@graph": [
    {
      "@type": "Article",
      "headline": "Construction Bid Prices vs Engineer's Estimates: 2017 to 2024 Trends",
      "description": "Winning bids on a fixed basket of Texas DOT items rose 68% between 2017 and 2024, and engineer's estimates ran about a year behind when prices jumped. This page follows the money from bid to final payment, including environmental line items.",
      "articleSection": "Project Cost Management",
      "publisher": {
        "@type": "Organization",
        "name": "Mastt",
        "url": "https://www.mastt.com",
        "logo": {
          "@type": "ImageObject",
          "url": "https://cdn.prod.website-files.com/607f739c92f9cfa16116b34d/607f978bbd76e475e2229a43_Mastt%20Logo.svg"
        }
      },
      "mainEntityOfPage": "https://www.mastt.com/research/construction-bid-price-and-estimate-trends"
    },
    {
      "@type": "Dataset",
      "name": "Construction Bid Prices vs Engineer's Estimates: 2017 to 2024 Trends",
      "description": "Records. Our own figures come from an internal Mastt study of public Texas DOT contract records: The price index starts from the unit prices on about 13,700 lettings from 2017 to 2024. It holds a fixed basket of 523 items priced per unit, each with at least 15 winning-bid prices in every year, which together appear on about 10,800 lettings. It combines each item's yearly median winning and estimated price using fixed weights: each item's winning-bid dollars for the bid index and estimate dollars for the estimate index, over 2017 to 2024 combined. The letting comparisons use 5,912 state-let construction lettings, with the low bid compared against the engineer's estimate, and the all-lettings view adds maintenance and local lettings. The price-history model prices each 2023 and 2024 letting from the median winning unit price of every item over the previous 12 months. The miss is the gap between a predicted total and the winning bid, counted the same whether over or under, as a percentage of the winning bid, and the median miss is the middle of those gaps.",
      "creator": {
        "@type": "Organization",
        "name": "Mastt",
        "url": "https://www.mastt.com"
      }
    },
    {
      "@type": "FAQPage",
      "mainEntity": [
        {
          "@type": "Question",
          "name": "How much did Texas DOT bid prices rise from 2017 to 2024?",
          "acceptedAnswer": {
            "@type": "Answer",
            "text": "A fixed basket of 523 Texas DOT bid items rose 68% between 2017 and 2024, from an index of 100 to 168.3 in winning bid prices. The engineer's estimate for the same items rose 70%, to 170.0."
          }
        },
        {
          "@type": "Question",
          "name": "Did winning bids come in above the engineer's estimate in 2022?",
          "acceptedAnswer": {
            "@type": "Answer",
            "text": "Yes. By 2022 the winning-bid index on the 523-item basket stood 12.7% above the estimate index for the same items, with both set to 100 in 2017, and the median state-let construction job was won 5.4% over the estimate. It was the only year from 2017 to 2024 when the median job was won above the estimate."
          }
        },
        {
          "@type": "Question",
          "name": "How many bidders does a Texas DOT construction job draw?",
          "acceptedAnswer": {
            "@type": "Answer",
            "text": "State-let construction jobs drew 4.7 bidders on average from 2017 to 2024. The average fell from 5.1 in 2021 to 3.9 in 2022, and single-bid jobs rose from 3.7% to 6.0% of jobs."
          }
        },
        {
          "@type": "Question",
          "name": "What share of bid lines are never used?",
          "acceptedAnswer": {
            "@type": "Answer",
            "text": "13.4% of bid lines, about 1 in 7, were never used on 5,265 completed Texas DOT construction contracts. Only 39.1% ended at exactly the bid quantity."
          }
        },
        {
          "@type": "Question",
          "name": "How much do contracts change between award and final payment?",
          "acceptedAnswer": {
            "@type": "Answer",
            "text": "Final payments ran 5.0% above award value on 5,265 completed construction contracts, but 12.8% of contract value moved across four payment categories, and 26.1% when each item is counted separately. New item types that were not in the bid added 7.5%, and 78% of jobs gained at least one."
          }
        },
        {
          "@type": "Question",
          "name": "How much of a Texas road job is environmental work?",
          "acceptedAnswer": {
            "@type": "Answer",
            "text": "Environmental items are on 81% of completed construction contracts and take a median 1.0% of final cost, or 1.6% of all construction dollars. Erosion control is on 79% of jobs and is typically paid 56% of its bid value."
          }
        },
        {
          "@type": "Question",
          "name": "How much does contamination cost compared with its bid?",
          "acceptedAnswer": {
            "@type": "Answer",
            "text": "Hazardous material items were bid at $1.3m in priced items and paid $35.4m on completed Texas DOT construction contracts, about 27 times the bid items. The contracts also held $7.6m of hazardous force account allowances at award, most set at token amounts of $1,000 or less, so payments were about four times the $8.9m carried at award. 92% of the money was paid through force account and change order lines rather than bid items, and five jobs account for 75% of it."
          }
        }
      ]
    }
  ]
}

## Step 2: Text block 1

Paste into the post body (Rich Text).

Every estimator knows the feeling of watching bids come in above the estimate and not knowing whether the market moved or the estimate was wrong. Texas DOT publishes enough bid and payment records to answer that for its own road and bridge work. Mastt tracked prices on the same items across eight years of lettings, then followed each contract from bid to final payment. Texas figures describe Texas DOT work only, and outside figures link to their source.

A letting is one tender round for a contract. A bid item is one priced line of a contract, such as a length of silt fence.</li><li>A typical-project result is the median: half the jobs sat above it and half below. A combined dollar result adds up the dollars on every job first, so large jobs count for more. The text says which one each figure is.</li></ul></div></div></div></div></div>

## Step 5: Text block 2

Paste into the post body (Rich Text).

## TL;DR

- Winning bid prices on the same 523 items rose **68%** from 2017 to 2024, most of it in 2022 and 2023.
- 2022 was the one year the typical job was won above the estimate, with unit prices running **12.7%** ahead.
- Bidders per job fell from **5.1 to 3.9** that year, and jobs with one bidder were won a median 17.6% over the estimate.
- Estimates took about **a year** to catch up, and in any year only about half land within 10% of the low bid.
- Only **39%** of bid lines finished at the bid quantity, and one in seven was never used.
- A **5% net change** after award hid 13% of contract value moving, and 78% of jobs gained a new item type.
- Environmental items sit on **81%** of jobs but take 1.6% of dollars, and hazardous work was paid 27 times its bid items.

 
## How far did Texas DOT bid prices rise from 2017 to 2024?

 By 68%. Winning bid prices on a fixed basket of 523 Texas DOT items rose from an index of 100 in 2017 to 168.3 in 2024, and the engineer's estimates for the same items rose 70%. Most of the rise came in 2022 and 2023.

 The basket holds items priced per unit that had at least 15 winning-bid prices in every year, leaves out lump sums, takes each item's median winning price for the year, and weights each item by its winning-bid dollars across all eight years. The weights stay fixed, so the index moves on price alone, not on what was bought.

---

style="font-size:13px;color:var(--text-muted);margin:12px 0 0">How far the winning-bid index sat above or below the estimate index: 2017 0.0%, 2018 -0.9%, 2019 +9.7%, 2020 +0.4%, 2021 +1.9%, 2022 +12.7%, 2023 +3.7%, 2024 -1.0%.</p></div></div></div></div><p class="mbpef01-cap">Figure 1: Winning bids and engineer's estimates on the same 523 items, 2017 = 100</p><p class="mbpef01-src">Source: Mastt analysis of public contract records, Texas DOT, 2017 to 2024</p><p class="mbpef01-src">Note: Each line shows prices on the same 523 items relative to 2017, which is set at 100, so 168 means prices were 68% higher. Each item's median price in a year is compared with its 2017 median, and the changes are averaged with each item weighted by its winning-bid dollars (estimate dollars for the estimate line). Weighting by 2017 dollars alone gives 167.5 for 2024 instead of 168.3, so the weighting period makes little difference.</p></div>
<script>(function(){var root=document.getElementById('mastt-bpe-f01');if(!root||root.getAttribute('data-mbpef01-on'))return;root.setAttribute('data-mbpef01-on','1');
var RM=!root.classList.contains('mbpef01-js');
var mxGen=0;
var mxZero=function(el){if(!el.hasAttribute('data-final'))el.setAttribute('data-final',el.textContent);var dec=+(el.getAttribute('data-dec')||0);el.textContent=(el.getAttribute('data-pre')||'')+(0).toFixed(dec)+(el.getAttribute('data-suf')||'');};
var mxCount=function(el,g){var to=parseFloat(el.getAttribute('data-to'));if(isNaN(to))return;var fin=el.getAttribute('data-final')||el.textContent,dec=+(el.getAttribute('data-dec')||0),pre=el.getAttribute('data-pre')||'',suf=el.getAttribute('data-suf')||'',f=new Intl.NumberFormat('en-US',{minimumFractionDigits:dec,maximumFractionDigits:dec}),t0=null,d=950;el.setAttribute('data-final',fin);var step=function(t){if(g!==mxGen)return;if(t0===null)t0=t;var p=Math.min(1,(t-t0)/d),e=1-Math.pow(1-p,3);el.textContent=pre+f.format(to*e)+suf;if(p<1)requestAnimationFrame(step);else el.textContent=fin;};requestAnimationFrame(step);};
var mxIn=function(){if(root.classList.contains('mbpef01-in'))return;mxGen++;var g=mxGen;root.classList.add('mbpef01-in');root.querySelectorAll('[data-to]').forEach(function(el){mxCount(el,g);});};
var mxOut=function(){if(!root.classList.contains('mbpef01-in'))return;mxGen++;root.classList.remove('mbpef01-in');root.querySelectorAll('[data-to]').forEach(mxZero);};
if(RM||!('IntersectionObserver' in window)){root.classList.add('mbpef01-in');}else{root.querySelectorAll('[data-to]').forEach(mxZero);var io=new IntersectionObserver(function(es){es.forEach(function(e){if(e.isIntersecting&&e.intersectionRatio>=.15){mxIn();}else if(!e.isIntersecting){mxOut();}});},{threshold:[0,.15]});io.observe(root);}
root.querySelectorAll('.mbpef01-tabs').forEach(function(box){var tabs=[].slice.call(box.querySelectorAll(':scope>.mbpef01-tablist>.mbpef01-tab')),panels=[].slice.call(box.querySelectorAll(':scope>.mbpef01-panel'));var pick=function(v,focus){tabs.forEach(function(t){var on=t.getAttribute('data-v')===v;t.setAttribute('aria-selected',on?'true':'false');t.tabIndex=on?0:-1;if(on&&focus)t.focus();});panels.forEach(function(p){p.hidden=p.getAttribute('data-v')!==v;});box.dispatchEvent(new CustomEvent('mbpef01-tab',{bubbles:true}));};tabs.forEach(function(t,i){t.addEventListener('click',function(){pick(t.getAttribute('data-v'));});t.addEventListener('keydown',function(e){var k=e.key,j=k==='ArrowRight'?i+1:k==='ArrowLeft'?i-1:-1;if(j<0&&k!=='ArrowLeft')return;e.preventDefault();var n=tabs[(j+tabs.length)%tabs.length];pick(n.getAttribute('data-v'),true);});});});
var mxTip=function(host){var tip=host.querySelector(':scope>.mbpef01-tip');if(!tip){tip=document.createElement('div');tip.className='mbpef01-tip';tip.setAttribute('aria-hidden','true');host.appendChild(tip);}return{show:function(x,y,h){tip.innerHTML=h;tip.style.left=x+'px';tip.style.top=y+'px';tip.classList.add('mbpef01-on');},hide:function(){tip.classList.remove('mbpef01-on');}};};
try{
root.querySelectorAll('.mbpef01-line').forEach(function(c){
var H=+c.getAttribute('data-h'),K=+c.getAttribute('data-k'),TOP=12,GAP=14,plot=c.querySelector('.mbpef01-line-plot'),tbl=c.querySelector('.mbpef01-line-t'),msg=c.querySelector('.mbpef01-line-msg');
var gs=[].slice.call(c.querySelectorAll('.mbpef01-line-s')),n=gs.length,sets=[].slice.call(c.querySelectorAll('.mbpef01-line-gs')),btns=[].slice.call(c.querySelectorAll('.mbpef01-line-tg')),labs=[].slice.call(c.querySelectorAll('.mbpef01-line-ax')),evs=[].slice.call(c.querySelectorAll('.mbpef01-line-ev'));
var vals=gs.map(function(g){return g.getAttribute('data-v').split(',').map(Number);}),last=(vals[0]||[]).length-1;
var sc=c.getAttribute('data-sc').split(';').map(function(s){return s.split(',').map(Number);}),map=c.getAttribute('data-map').split(',').map(Number);
var mask=+c.getAttribute('data-mask'),cur=sc[+c.getAttribute('data-start')]||[0,1],lo=cur[0],hi=cur[1],pres=gs.map(function(_,i){return mask&(1<<i)?1:0;});
var lines=gs.map(function(g){return g.querySelector('.mbpef01-line-l');}),areas=gs.map(function(g){return g.querySelector('.mbpef01-line-a');}),bases=lines.map(function(l){return l.getAttribute('d');});
var sw=function(g){return g.style.getPropertyValue('--s').replace(/["<>]/g,'');},isDash=function(g){return lines[gs.indexOf(g)].classList.contains('mbpef01-line-dash');};
var ix=-1,tw=null;
var names=tbl?[].slice.call(tbl.tHead.rows[0].cells,1).map(function(t){return t.innerHTML;}):[];var plain=tbl?[].slice.call(tbl.tHead.rows[0].cells,1).map(function(t){return t.textContent;}):[];
var cross=document.createElement('div');cross.className='mbpef01-line-x';cross.setAttribute('aria-hidden','true');
var dots=gs.map(function(g){var d=document.createElement('div');d.className='mbpef01-line-d';d.style.setProperty('--s',sw(g));d.setAttribute('aria-hidden','true');return d;});
var tip=document.createElement('div');tip.className='mbpef01-line-tip';tip.setAttribute('aria-hidden','true');
plot.appendChild(cross);dots.forEach(function(d){plot.appendChild(d);});plot.appendChild(tip);
plot.setAttribute('role','slider');plot.setAttribute('aria-orientation','horizontal');plot.setAttribute('aria-valuemin','1');plot.setAttribute('aria-valuemax',String(Math.max(1,last+1)));
var sum=plot.getAttribute('aria-label');plot.setAttribute('aria-label',c.getAttribute('data-label')+', explore by '+c.getAttribute('data-cat'));
var q=function(){return (H-TOP)/((hi-lo)||1);};
var yOf=function(i,v){return H-q()*Math.max(pres[i],1e-4)*(v-lo);};
var text=function(i){var r=tbl&&tbl.tBodies[0].rows[i];return r?r:null;};
var cnt=function(){var k=0;for(var i=0;i<n;i++)if(mask&(1<<i))k++;return k;};
var describe=function(){var r=ix<0?null:text(ix);if(!r)return cnt()+' of '+n+' series shown';var o=r.cells[0].textContent+': ';var b=[];for(var i=0;i<n;i++)if(mask&(1<<i))b.push(plain[i]+' '+r.cells[i+1].textContent);return o+b.join(', ');};
var spreadY=function(ys,gap,a,b){var idx=ys.map(function(_,i){return i;}).sort(function(p,r){return ys[p]-ys[r];}),out=ys.slice(),k,i;for(k=0;k<idx.length;k++){i=idx[k];out[i]=Math.max(out[i],k?out[idx[k-1]]+gap:a);}for(k=idx.length-1;k>=0;k--){i=idx[k];out[i]=Math.min(out[i],k===idx.length-1?b:out[idx[k+1]]-gap);}return out;};
var ends=function(){if(last<0||!evs.length)return;var vis=[],ys=[],i;for(i=0;i<n;i++)if(mask&(1<<i)){vis.push(i);ys.push(yOf(i,vals[i][last]));}var sp=spreadY(ys,22,TOP,H-TOP);vis.forEach(function(j,k){evs[j].style.top=sp[k]+'px';});evs.forEach(function(e,j){e.classList.toggle('mbpef01-off',!(mask&(1<<j)));});};
var place=function(){
if(ix<0)return;var w=plot.clientWidth,x=last>0?ix/last:.5,cx=Math.round(x*w)+.5;
cross.style.left=cx+'px';var ys=[];
dots.forEach(function(d,i){var on=(mask&(1<<i))!==0;d.classList.toggle('mbpef01-off',!on);d.style.left=cx+'px';var y=yOf(i,vals[i][ix]);d.style.top=y+'px';if(on)ys.push(y);});
var r=text(ix);if(r){var h='<span class="mbpef01-line-tt">'+r.cells[0].innerHTML+'</span>';for(var i=0;i<n;i++)if(mask&(1<<i))h+='<span class="mbpef01-line-tr" style="--s:'+sw(gs[i])+'"><span class="mbpef01-line-ts'+(isDash(gs[i])?' mbpef01-line-dash':'')+'"></span><span class="mbpef01-line-tn">'+names[i]+'</span><span class="mbpef01-line-tv">'+r.cells[i+1].innerHTML+'</span></span>';tip.innerHTML=h;}
var bw=tip.offsetWidth,bh=tip.offsetHeight,tx=cx+GAP;if(tx+bw>w)tx=cx-GAP-bw;tx=Math.max(0,Math.min(tx,Math.max(0,w-bw)));
var mid=ys.length?ys.reduce(function(a,b){return a+b;},0)/ys.length:H/2,ty=Math.max(0,Math.min(mid-bh/2,Math.max(0,H-bh)));
tip.style.transform='translate('+tx+'px,'+ty+'px)';
plot.setAttribute('aria-valuenow',String(ix+1));plot.setAttribute('aria-valuetext',describe());};
var paint=function(){var qq=q();
gs.forEach(function(g,i){var p=Math.max(pres[i],1e-4);g.setAttribute('transform','matrix(1 0 0 '+(-qq*p/K)+' 0 '+(H+qq*p*lo)+')');g.style.opacity=pres[i]<.001?0:pres[i];if(areas[i])areas[i].setAttribute('d',bases[i]+'L1000 0L0 0Z');});
ends();place();};
var targetPres=function(){return gs.map(function(_,i){return mask&(1<<i)?1:0;});};
var go=function(){var si=n<=5?map[mask]:-1,to=si>=0?sc[si]:[lo,hi];
if(si>=0)sets.forEach(function(s,k){s.classList.toggle('mbpef01-on',k===si);});
msg.hidden=mask!==0;
var f0=lo,f1=hi,fp=pres.slice(),tp=targetPres();
if(tw)cancelAnimationFrame(tw);

---

## Step 7: Text block 3

Paste into the post body (Rich Text).

- Prices dipped in 2018, rose to 109.0 in 2019 and held between 107 and 109 through 2021.
- Winning bids jumped 23.4% in 2022 and a further 17.0% in 2023. Estimates rose 11.6% in 2022 and 27.1% in 2023.
- In 2024 the two series crossed, with estimates 1.0% above winning bids.
- A smaller version of the same pattern showed in 2019, when winning bids ran 9.7% ahead of estimates before the gap closed the next year.

 
### Input costs moved first, Texas bids followed a year later

---

.mbpef02-line-ev{opacity:1;transition:opacity .5s ease .85s,filter .2s ease}@media (max-width:560px){#mastt-bpe-f02 .mbpef02-line-ch{padding:0 54px 0 34px}#mastt-bpe-f02 class="mbpef02-cap">Figure 2: Texas winning bids against two national indices, 2017 = 100</p><p class="mbpef02-src">Source: Mastt analysis of public contract records, Texas DOT; FHWA National Highway Construction Cost Index; BLS Producer Price Index, inputs to highways and streets</p><p class="mbpef02-src">Note: All three series are set to 100 in 2017 so their growth can be compared directly. The national bid index is published quarterly and the input cost index monthly, so each year's point is the average of that year's published values.</p></div>
<script>(function(){var root=document.getElementById('mastt-bpe-f02');if(!root||root.getAttribute('data-mbpef02-on'))return;root.setAttribute('data-mbpef02-on','1');
var RM=!root.classList.contains('mbpef02-js');
var mxGen=0;
var mxZero=function(el){if(!el.hasAttribute('data-final'))el.setAttribute('data-final',el.textContent);var dec=+(el.getAttribute('data-dec')||0);el.textContent=(el.getAttribute('data-pre')||'')+(0).toFixed(dec)+(el.getAttribute('data-suf')||'');};
var mxCount=function(el,g){var to=parseFloat(el.getAttribute('data-to'));if(isNaN(to))return;var fin=el.getAttribute('data-final')||el.textContent,dec=+(el.getAttribute('data-dec')||0),pre=el.getAttribute('data-pre')||'',suf=el.getAttribute('data-suf')||'',f=new Intl.NumberFormat('en-US',{minimumFractionDigits:dec,maximumFractionDigits:dec}),t0=null,d=950;el.setAttribute('data-final',fin);var step=function(t){if(g!==mxGen)return;if(t0===null)t0=t;var p=Math.min(1,(t-t0)/d),e=1-Math.pow(1-p,3);el.textContent=pre+f.format(to*e)+suf;if(p<1)requestAnimationFrame(step);else el.textContent=fin;};requestAnimationFrame(step);};
var mxIn=function(){if(root.classList.contains('mbpef02-in'))return;mxGen++;var g=mxGen;root.classList.add('mbpef02-in');root.querySelectorAll('[data-to]').forEach(function(el){mxCount(el,g);});};
var mxOut=function(){if(!root.classList.contains('mbpef02-in'))return;mxGen++;root.classList.remove('mbpef02-in');root.querySelectorAll('[data-to]').forEach(mxZero);};
if(RM||!('IntersectionObserver' in window)){root.classList.add('mbpef02-in');}else{root.querySelectorAll('[data-to]').forEach(mxZero);var io=new IntersectionObserver(function(es){es.forEach(function(e){if(e.isIntersecting&&e.intersectionRatio>=.15){mxIn();}else if(!e.isIntersecting){mxOut();}});},{threshold:[0,.15]});io.observe(root);}
root.querySelectorAll('.mbpef02-tabs').forEach(function(box){var tabs=[].slice.call(box.querySelectorAll(':scope>.mbpef02-tablist>.mbpef02-tab')),panels=[].slice.call(box.querySelectorAll(':scope>.mbpef02-panel'));var pick=function(v,focus){tabs.forEach(function(t){var on=t.getAttribute('data-v')===v;t.setAttribute('aria-selected',on?'true':'false');t.tabIndex=on?0:-1;if(on&&focus)t.focus();});panels.forEach(function(p){p.hidden=p.getAttribute('data-v')!==v;});box.dispatchEvent(new CustomEvent('mbpef02-tab',{bubbles:true}));};tabs.forEach(function(t,i){t.addEventListener('click',function(){pick(t.getAttribute('data-v'));});t.addEventListener('keydown',function(e){var k=e.key,j=k==='ArrowRight'?i+1:k==='ArrowLeft'?i-1:-1;if(j<0&&k!=='ArrowLeft')return;e.preventDefault();var n=tabs[(j+tabs.length)%tabs.length];pick(n.getAttribute('data-v'),true);});});});
var mxTip=function(host){var tip=host.querySelector(':scope>.mbpef02-tip');if(!tip){tip=document.createElement('div');tip.className='mbpef02-tip';tip.setAttribute('aria-hidden','true');host.appendChild(tip);}return{show:function(x,y,h){tip.innerHTML=h;tip.style.left=x+'px';tip.style.top=y+'px';tip.classList.add('mbpef02-on');},hide:function(){tip.classList.remove('mbpef02-on');}};};
try{
root.querySelectorAll('.mbpef02-line').forEach(function(c){
var H=+c.getAttribute('data-h'),K=+c.getAttribute('data-k'),TOP=12,GAP=14,plot=c.querySelector('.mbpef02-line-plot'),tbl=c.querySelector('.mbpef02-line-t'),msg=c.querySelector('.mbpef02-line-msg');
var gs=[].slice.call(c.querySelectorAll('.mbpef02-line-s')),n=gs.length,sets=[].slice.call(c.querySelectorAll('.mbpef02-line-gs')),btns=[].slice.call(c.querySelectorAll('.mbpef02-line-tg')),labs=[].slice.call(c.querySelectorAll('.mbpef02-line-ax')),evs=[].slice.call(c.querySelectorAll('.mbpef02-line-ev'));
var vals=gs.map(function(g){return g.getAttribute('data-v').split(',').map(Number);}),last=(vals[0]||[]).length-1;
var sc=c.getAttribute('data-sc').split(';').map(function(s){return s.split(',').map(Number);}),map=c.getAttribute('data-map').split(',').map(Number);
var mask=+c.getAttribute('data-mask'),cur=sc[+c.getAttribute('data-start')]||[0,1],lo=cur[0],hi=cur[1],pres=gs.map(function(_,i){return mask&(1<<i)?1:0;});
var lines=gs.map(function(g){return g.querySelector('.mbpef02-line-l');}),areas=gs.map(function(g){return g.querySelector('.mbpef02-line-a');}),bases=lines.map(function(l){return l.getAttribute('d');});
var sw=function(g){return g.style.getPropertyValue('--s').replace(/["<>]/g,'');},isDash=function(g){return lines[gs.indexOf(g)].classList.contains('mbpef02-line-dash');};
var ix=-1,tw=null;
var names=tbl?[].slice.call(tbl.tHead.rows[0].cells,1).map(function(t){return t.innerHTML;}):[];var plain=tbl?[].slice.call(tbl.tHead.rows[0].cells,1).map(function(t){return t.textContent;}):[];
var cross=document.createElement('div');cross.className='mbpef02-line-x';cross.setAttribute('aria-hidden','true');
var dots=gs.map(function(g){var d=document.createElement('div');d.className='mbpef02-line-d';d.style.setProperty('--s',sw(g));d.setAttribute('aria-hidden','true');return d;});
var tip=document.createElement('div');tip.className='mbpef02-line-tip';tip.setAttribute('aria-hidden','true');
plot.appendChild(cross);dots.forEach(function(d){plot.appendChild(d);});plot.appendChild(tip);
plot.setAttribute('role','slider');plot.setAttribute('aria-orientation','horizontal');plot.setAttribute('aria-valuemin','1');plot.setAttribute('aria-valuemax',String(Math.max(1,last+1)));
var sum=plot.getAttribute('aria-label');plot.setAttribute('aria-label',c.getAttribute('data-label')+', explore by '+c.getAttribute('data-cat'));
var q=function(){return (H-TOP)/((hi-lo)||1);};
var yOf=function(i,v){return H-q()*Math.max(pres[i],1e-4)*(v-lo);};
var text=function(i){var r=tbl&&tbl.tBodies[0].rows[i];return r?r:null;};
var cnt=function(){var k=0;for(var i=0;i<n;i++)if(mask&(1<<i))k++;return k;};
var describe=function(){var r=ix<0?null:text(ix);if(!r)return cnt()+' of '+n+' series shown';var o=r.cells[0].textContent+': ';var b=[];for(var i=0;i<n;i++)if(mask&(1<<i))b.push(plain[i]+' '+r.cells[i+1].textContent);return o+b.join(', ');};
var spreadY=function(ys,gap,a,b){var idx=ys.map(function(_,i){return i;}).sort(function(p,r){return ys[p]-ys[r];}),out=ys.slice(),k,i;for(k=0;k<idx.length;k++){i=idx[k];out[i]=Math.max(out[i],k?out[idx[k-1]]+gap:a);}for(k=idx.length-1;k>=0;k--){i=idx[k];out[i]=Math.min(out[i],k===idx.length-1?b:out[idx[k+1]]-gap);}return out;};
var ends=function(){if(last<0||!evs.length)return;var vis=[],ys=[],i;for(i=0;i<n;i++)if(mask&(1<<i)){vis.push(i);ys.push(yOf(i,vals[i][last]));}var sp=spreadY(ys,22,TOP,H-TOP);vis.forEach(function(j,k){evs[j].style.top=sp[k]+'px';});evs.forEach(function(e,j){e.classList.toggle('mbpef02-off',!(mask&(1<<j)));});};
var place=function(){
if(ix<0)return;var w=plot.clientWidth,x=last>0?ix/last:.5,cx=Math.round(x*w)+.5;
cross.style.left=cx+'px';var ys=[];
dots.forEach(function(d,i){var on=(mask&(1<<i))!==0;d.classList.toggle('mbpef02-off',!on);d.style.left=cx+'px';var y=yOf(i,vals[i][ix]);d.style.top=y+'px';if(on)ys.push(y);});
var r=text(ix);if(r){var h='<span class="mbpef02-line-tt">'+r.cells[0].innerHTML+'</span>';for(var i=0;i<n;i++)if(mask&(1<<i))h+='<span class="mbpef02-line-tr" style="--s:'+sw(gs[i])+'"><span class="mbpef02-line-ts'+(isDash(gs[i])?' mbpef02-line-dash':'')+'"></span><span class="mbpef02-line-tn">'+names[i]+'</span><span class="mbpef02-line-tv">'+r.cells[i+1].innerHTML+'</span></span>';tip.innerHTML=h;}
var bw=tip.offsetWidth,bh=tip.offsetHeight,tx=cx+GAP;if(tx+bw>w)tx=cx-GAP-bw;tx=Math.max(0,Math.min(tx,Math.max(0,w-bw)));
var mid=ys.length?ys.reduce(function(a,b){return a+b;},0)/ys.length:H/2,ty=Math.max(0,Math.min(mid-bh/2,Math.max(0,H-bh)));
tip.style.transform='translate('+tx+'px,'+ty+'px)';
plot.setAttribute('aria-valuenow',String(ix+1));plot.setAttribute('aria-valuetext',describe());};
var paint=function(){var qq=q();
gs.forEach(function(g,i){var p=Math.max(pres[i],1e-4);g.setAttribute('transform','matrix(1 0 0 '+(-qq*p/K)+' 0 '+(H+qq*p*lo)+')');g.style.opacity=pres[i]<.001?0:pres[i];if(areas[i])areas[i].setAttribute('d',bases[i]+'L1000 0L0 0Z');});
ends();place();};
var targetPres=function(){return gs.map(function(_,i){return mask&(1<<i)?1:0;});};
var go=function(){var si=n<=5?map[mask]:-1,to=si>=0?sc[si]:[lo,hi];
if(si>=0)sets.forEach(function(s,k){s.classList.toggle('mbpef02-on',k===si);});
msg.hidden=mask!==0;
var f0=lo,f1=hi,fp=pres.slice(),tp=targetPres();
if(tw)cancelAnimationFrame(tw);
if(RM||!window.requestAnimationFrame){lo=to[0];hi=to[1];pres=tp;paint();return;}

---

## Step 9: Text block 4

Paste into the post body (Rich Text).

- The US Bureau of Labor Statistics [producer price index for inputs to highway and street construction](https://fred.stlouisfed.org/series/WPUIP231231) rose 46% over the same years: up 17.2% in 2021, 16.1% in 2022, then 0.3% and 0.5%.
- Texas winning bids barely moved in 2021 (+0.5%) and rose in 2022 and 2023, consistent with bid prices following input costs by about a year, although the records cannot show that one caused the other.
- The Federal Highway Administration's [National Highway Construction Cost Index](https://www.fhwa.dot.gov/policy/otps/nhcci/methodology.cfm) rose 93% from 2017 to 2024 and 26.3% in 2022 alone, close to the 23.4% rise in the Texas basket that year.
- The national index pools bids from many states, keeps every item and updates its weights each period, so the 68% against 93% gap over the full period cannot be read as Texas prices rising less than prices elsewhere.

 In Australia the ABS producer price indexes play the same role. The [June quarter 2026 release](https://www.abs.gov.au/statistics/economy/price-indexes-and-inflation/producer-price-indexes-australia/jun-2026) showed heavy and civil engineering construction prices up 3.6% over twelve months, with road and bridge construction up 4.5% in the quarter on higher bitumen and diesel prices.

 **What this means for project teams:** Input prices and bid prices can move a year apart, so a general inflation rate is a poor guide. Choose an escalation index that matches the work you are buying, build a basket of the items you buy most, and test it against your own last few tenders.

 
## Did winning bids run ahead of the engineer's estimate in 2022?

 Yes. In 2022 the median state-let construction job was won 5.4% over the engineer's estimate, the only year from 2017 to 2024 when the typical winning bid came in above it.

var mxOut=function(){if(!root.classList.contains('mbpef03-in'))return;mxGen++;root.classList.remove('mbpef03-in');root.querySelectorAll('[data-to]').forEach(mxZero);};

---

## Step 11: Text block 5

Paste into the post body (Rich Text).

- The median winning bid was 4.7% under the estimate in 2021 and 5.4% over in 2022. Across 2017 to 2024 the yearly median ran between 7.5% under and 5.4% over.
- 59.9% of 2022 state-let construction jobs were won above the estimate, against 26% to 48% in the other years.
- Nearly one 2022 job in four (22.6%) was won at least 20% above the estimate, against 4.8% to 14.8% in other years.
- That 20% line matters. The Texas DOT design manual calls for a [written justification](https://www.txdot.gov/manuals/des/pse/chapter-8--post-letting-guidelines/section-2--letting-overrun-underrun-justification.html) when the low bid is 20% or more over or under the estimate, or more than 10% either side with a single bidder.

It is the ratio of the two indices, and it compares growth since 2017, not the price level in 2022.</li><li><strong>Item price level, 11.7%:</strong> Measured directly in 2022, the median winning unit price on those items was 11.7% above the median estimate unit price, weighted by winning-bid dollars. In 2017 the two were almost level, with winning prices 0.2% below.</li><li><strong>Whole jobs, 5.4%:</strong> The median 2022 job was won 5.4% over its total estimate. This covers every item on the job, including lump sums, and describes the middle job rather than prices on a fixed set of items. The records do not show which items account for the difference.</li></ul></div></div></div></div></div>

---

## Step 13: Text block 6

Paste into the post body (Rich Text).

**What this means for project teams:** Track the share of tenders won 20% or more over the estimate, not only the average. In 2022 that share reached 22.6% of Texas DOT state-let construction jobs, against 4.8% to 14.8% in other years. Set the review trigger before bids open and record the reason for each large variance, as the Texas DOT manual requires.

 
## Did fewer bidders push prices up?

 The two moved together. Bidder numbers fell in 2022 just as winning bids rose, and across all eight years jobs with fewer bidders were won further above the estimate. The records cannot prove that fewer bidders caused the higher prices.

---

## Step 15: Text block 7

Paste into the post body (Rich Text).

- Bidders per job fell from 5.1 in 2021 to 3.9 in 2022 and 3.7 in 2023, against about 5.1 a job over 2017 to 2021.
- Single-bid jobs rose from 3.7% of jobs in 2021 to 6.0% in 2022 and 6.7% in 2023, roughly double their 3.2% average over 2017 to 2021.
- Bidders recovered to 4.4 a job in 2024.

 
### Winning bids fall as bidder numbers rise

---

---

## Step 17: Text block 8

Paste into the post body (Rich Text).

- One bidder meant a median 17.6% over the estimate, two bidders 5.3% over, three 0.8% under, four 3.7% under, five 5.9% under and six or more 9.1% under.
- The spread inside each group is wide. For single-bid jobs the middle half of winning bids ran from 7.1% to 34.9% over the estimate.
- Job size does not explain it. Single-bid jobs had a median estimate of $2.29m against $2.28m for jobs with six or more bidders.
- Work type, location and timing may still play a part, and the records do not show why fewer firms bid in 2022.

 **What this means for project teams:** Use the bidder-group medians as a benchmark for how far a thin tender can move, not as a fixed premium: one or two bidders meant a median 5% to 18% over the estimate, with a wide spread around it. Before you rely on these figures, check how many bidders similar jobs in your market drew recently.

 
## How long did estimates take to catch up, and how close do they get?

 About a year. Estimates built from past bid prices trail a moving market, and the gap that opened in 2022 had mostly closed by 2023. On individual jobs, only about half of estimates land within 10% of the low bid in any year.

 - On the Texas basket, the winning-bid index sat 12.7% above the estimate index in 2022, 3.7% above in 2023 and 1.0% below in 2024.
- FHWA's [2015 national review of state cost estimation practice](https://www.fhwa.dot.gov/construction/contracts/2014cost.pdf) saw the same lag in reverse: in the 2008 to 2010 downturn, bids ran 10% to 30% below state estimates because estimates lagged market conditions.

 
### About half of estimates land within 10% of the low bid

 - FHWA's guidance says an estimate should be within 10% of the low bid for at least half of projects. The 2015 review found 48% of 1,611 projects within that band nationally.
- Texas DOT's own [internal audit](https://ftp.txdot.gov/pub/txdot-info/aud/reports/Plan_Review_and_Bid_Estimation_Effectiveness_Final_Internal_Audit_Report.pdf) tested 842 completed projects in eight districts, let from 2016 to August 2020, and found yearly averages from 42% to 53% against the 50% goal.
- Mastt's analysis of every district found 45% to 53% of state-let construction lettings within 10% in each year from 2017 to 2022, then 44.0% in 2023 and 39.5% in 2024. One year is too short to call a trend.
- The [US Department of Transportation Inspector General](https://www.oversight.gov/reports/audit/fhwa-lacks-adequate-oversight-and-guidance-engineers-estimates) found in 2019 that FHWA lacked adequate guidance to oversee the accuracy of engineer's estimates, so this is a national question as well as a Texas one.

 
### A price-history model missed by more than the engineer's estimate

 Mastt priced each 2023 and 2024 state-let construction letting from the median winning price of every item over the previous 12 months, using only data that existed before the letting, and compared both it and the engineer's estimate with the winning bid.

---

## Step 19: Text block 9

Paste into the post body (Rich Text).

- The engineer's estimate missed the winning bid by a median 12.8% across 1,585 lettings. The model missed by 16.7%.
- The model came closer than the engineer on 38.4% of lettings, and a 50/50 blend of the two missed by 13.4%, no better than the estimate alone.
- Item price history captures market prices but not location, staging, traffic and site conditions, which the engineer can allow for. Any estimating model has to beat 12.8% on this measure.

 **What this means for project teams:** When prices are rising, estimates built on last year's bids will be low. Add escalation in the quarter prices start to move, and compare the estimate with the latest bids rather than waiting for the next scheduled review. Even in steadier years, plan for about half of tenders to land more than 10% from the estimate.

 
## How often are bid quantities right?

 Not often. Only 39.1% of bid lines on completed Texas DOT construction contracts finished at exactly the bid quantity, and one in seven (13.4%) was never used.

 Prices are half of contract value and quantities are the other half. Mastt joined 341,637 bid lines on 5,265 completed construction contracts to the quantity finally paid on each line.

<script>(function(){var root=document.getElementById('mastt-bpe-f06');if(!root||root.getAttribute('data-mbpef06-on'))return;root.setAttribute('data-mbpef06-on','1');

var mxGen=0;
var mxZero=function(el){if(!el.hasAttribute('data-final'))el.setAttribute('data-final',el.textContent);var dec=+(el.getAttribute('data-dec')||0);el.textContent=(el.getAttribute('data-pre')||'')+(0).toFixed(dec)+(el.getAttribute('data-suf')||'');};
var mxCount=function(el,g){var to=parseFloat(el.getAttribute('data-to'));if(isNaN(to))return;var fin=el.getAttribute('data-final')||el.textContent,dec=+(el.getAttribute('data-dec')||0),pre=el.getAttribute('data-pre')||'',suf=el.getAttribute('data-suf')||'',f=new Intl.NumberFormat('en-US',{minimumFractionDigits:dec,maximumFractionDigits:dec}),t0=null,d=950;el.setAttribute('data-final',fin);var step=function(t){if(g!==mxGen)return;if(t0===null)t0=t;var p=Math.min(1,(t-t0)/d),e=1-Math.pow(1-p,3);el.textContent=pre+f.format(to*e)+suf;if(p<1)requestAnimationFrame(step);else el.textContent=fin;};requestAnimationFrame(step);};
var mxIn=function(){if(root.classList.contains('mbpef06-in'))return;mxGen++;var g=mxGen;root.classList.add('mbpef06-in');root.querySelectorAll('[data-to]').forEach(function(el){mxCount(el,g);});};

---

## Step 21: Text block 10

Paste into the post body (Rich Text).

- 13.4% of bid lines were paid nothing, and another 17.2% were paid less than 90% of the bid quantity. Together that is 30.6% of lines.
- 12.8% finished within 10% of the bid quantity without matching it, and 39.1% matched it exactly.
- 17.6% were paid more than 110% of the bid quantity.

 
### Temporary and erosion-control items are the most over-quantified

---

## Step 23: Text block 11

Paste into the post body (Rich Text).

- Vegetative watering was never used on 58% of the bid lines that included it. Construction exits and temporary seeding were never used on 59% and 54%.
- Erosion and sediment control items under Item 506 used a median 40% of bid quantity, erosion-control seeding under Item 164 used 43%, and rock filter dams 36%. Silt fence, at 67%, was the highest in the table.
- Erosion control is a median 0.3% of a contract's final cost, so its lines fall below the [Texas DOT change-order test](https://www.txdot.gov/manuals/cst/cah/changes_to_the_contract/change_orders-ceghciif.html) for major items, which covers items worth at least 5% of the contract or $100,000 that move more than 25% from the bid quantity.

 The records show the gap, not the reason. These quantities depend on weather, staging and how the contractor works, so designers allow generous amounts, and owners pay only for what goes in. Either way, high quantities can distort bid comparison and hide how much is installed.

 **What this means for project teams:** Check temporary and erosion-control quantities before tender, using paid quantities from your last jobs rather than the design allowance. When you compare bids, look at unit prices on these high-quantity items as well as the totals.

 
## Where does the money move between award and final payment?

 Mostly under the net figure. On 5,265 completed Texas DOT construction contracts with $30.1bn awarded, final payments ran 5.0% above award value while at least 12.8% of contract value moved underneath.

(function(){
var GAP=0.5,pf=new Intl.NumberFormat('en-US',{style:'percent',maximumFractionDigits:0}),
share=function(s){return s>0&&s<.01?'<1%':pf.format(s);},
num=/^([^\d-]*)(-?[\d,]*\.?\d+)(.*)$/,
vfocus=function(el){try{return el.matches(':focus-visible');}catch(e){return true;}},
step=function(k,i,len){return({ArrowDown:i+1,ArrowRight:i+1,ArrowUp:i-1,ArrowLeft:i-1,Home:0,End:len-1})[k];},

var D=JSON.parse(box.getAttribute('data-d')),n=D.v.length;if(!n)return;

'+D.u:'',hid=D.h.map(Boolean),pin=D.p,pre=-1,cur=pin,shown=pin,tc=0;
var tot=function(){var t=0;D.v.forEach(function(v,i){if(!hid[i])t+=v;});return t;};
var mask=function(){var m=0;if(D.ac==='toggle')hid.forEach(function(h,i){if(h)m|=1<<i;});return m;};
var total=function(){if(T)return T[mask()];var m=num.exec(D.f),d=(m[2].split('.')[1]||'').length;nf=nf||new Intl.NumberFormat('en-US',{minimumFractionDigits:d,maximumFractionDigits:d});return m[1]+nf.format(tot())+m[3];};
var desc=function(i){return D.L[i]+', '+D.V[i]+sf+', '+share(D.v[i]/(tot()||1))+((D.m&&D.m[i])||'');};
var paint=function(){var t=tot()||1,at=0,g=hid.filter(function(h){return!h;}).length>1?GAP:0;segs.forEach(function(s,i){var l=hid[i]?0:D.v[i]/t*100,d=Math.max(0,l-g);s.style.setProperty('--a',+d.toFixed(2));s.style.setProperty('--r',+(100-d).toFixed(2));s.style.setProperty('--o',+(-(at+g/2)).toFixed(2));at+=l;});};
var texts=function(){var t=tot();rows.forEach(function(r,i){r.querySelector('.mbpef08-dn-rs').textContent=share(hid[i]||!t?0:D.v[i]/t);});box.setAttribute('data-sh',hid.some(Boolean)?'1':sh0);};
var show=function(){var k=cur,t=tot(),v=k<0?total():D.V[k],a=k<0?D.tl:D.L[k],m=k<0?(D.u&&D.tl.toLowerCase().indexOf(D.u.toLowerCase())<0?D.u:''):(box.getAttribute('data-sh')==='1'?share(D.v[k]/(t||1))+' of total':'');
cv.textContent=v;tag(cv,v);cl.textContent=a;cm.textContent=m||'\u00a0';ring.style.setProperty('--ct',k<0?'':getComputedStyle(segs[k]).getPropertyValue('--t'));
if(k!==shown&&!RM){ctr.classList.remove('mbpef08-roll');void ctr.offsetWidth;ctr.classList.add('mbpef08-roll');}shown=k;};
var update=function(){var k=[pre,pin].filter(function(i){return i>=0&&!hid[i];})[0];cur=k===undefined?-1:k;
ring.classList.toggle('mbpef08-act',cur>=0);
segs.forEach(function(s,i){s.classList.toggle('mbpef08-on',i===cur);});
rows.forEach(function(r,i){r.classList.toggle('mbpef08-on',i===cur);r.classList.toggle('mbpef08-pin',i===pin);r.classList.toggle('mbpef08-off',hid[i]);r.setAttribute('aria-pressed',String(D.ac==='toggle'?!hid[i]:pin===i));});
show();};
var flip=function(i){var was=hid[i];if(!was&&hid.filter(function(h){return!h;}).length<=1)return;hid[i]=!was;if(!tc){tc=1;segs.forEach(function(s){s.style.setProperty('--d','0s');});}paint();texts();update();live.textContent=D.L[i]+(was?' shown':' hidden')+'. '+D.tl+' '+total()+sf+'.';};
segs.forEach(function(s,i){s.addEventListener('pointerenter',function(e){if(e.pointerType==='mouse'&&!hid[i]){pre=i;update();}});s.addEventListener('click',function(){if(!hid[i]){pin=pin===i?-1:i;update();}});});
svg.addEventListener('pointerleave',function(e){if(e.pointerType==='mouse'&&pre>=0){pre=-1;update();}});
if(rows.length){
var ul=box.querySelector('.mbpef08-dn-legend');
ul.addEventListener('pointerleave',function(){if(pre>=0){pre=-1;update();}});
rows.forEach(function(r,i){
r.addEventListener('click',function(){if(D.ac==='toggle')flip(i);else{pin=pin===i?-1:i;update();}});
r.addEventListener('pointerenter',function(e){if(e.pointerType==='mouse'){pre=hid[i]?-1:i;update();}});
r.addEventListener('focus',function(){if(vfocus(r)){pre=hid[i]?-1:i;update();}});
r.addEventListener('blur',function(e){if(!(e.relatedTarget&&ul.contains(e.relatedTarget))){pre=-1;update();}});
r.addEventListener('keydown',function(e){if(e.key==='Escape'&&pin>=0){e.preventDefault();pin=-1;update();return;}var k=step(e.key,i,n);if(k===undefined)return;e.preventDefault();rows[(k+n)%n].focus();});});
}else{
ring.addEventListener('blur',function(){pre=-1;update();});
ring.addEventListener('keydown',function(e){var vis=[];hid.forEach(function(h,i){if(!h)vis.push(i);});if(!vis.length)return;
if(e.key==='Escape'){e.preventDefault();pre=-1;pin=-1;update();return;}
if((e.key==='Enter'||e.key===' ')&&cur>=0){e.preventDefault();pin=pin===cur?-1:cur;update();return;}
var ix=vis.indexOf(cur),k=step(e.key,ix<0?(/Up$|Left$/.test(e.key)?0:-1):ix,vis.length);if(k===undefined)return;e.preventDefault();pre=vis[(k+vis.length)%vis.length];update();live.textContent=desc(pre);});
}
});

---

## Step 26: Text block 12

Paste into the post body (Rich Text).

- Original bid items were paid 3.9% below award value, after overruns and underruns on those items are netted against each other.
- Item types that were not in the bid added 7.5%, price and payment adjustments 0.8%, and force account work 0.6%. Force account is work paid at cost plus a set markup, usually because no bid item fits.
- Added without cancelling, the four categories come to 12.8%. Counted item by item, with each original item's rise or fall kept separate, movement comes to 26.1% of award value.

 
### Most new-item spending was on standard items, often variants of work already bid

 - New item types added $2.27bn across the 5,265 contracts, and ordinary standard or special-specification items made up $1.46bn of it, spread over 4,313 item codes on 3,296 jobs.
- The five largest were paving and base items: asphalt paving mixtures (Item 344, $97.1m on 93 jobs), flexible pavement structure repair (Item 351, $93.5m on 257 jobs), asphalt mixtures under special specification 3077 ($93.2m on 116 jobs), continuously reinforced concrete pavement (Item 360, $73.8m on 47 jobs) and flexible base (Item 247, $64.0m on 119 jobs). Pavement markings (Item 666) were the most widespread, at $60.6m on 762 jobs.
- For 66% of these dollars, the same specification item had already been bid on that contract under a different code, so much of the spending was on variants of work already in the contract.
- The remaining $812m ran through placeholder codes: generic change order lines ($585m across 2,317 jobs), police traffic control, the largest single line at $152m across 2,509 jobs, and about $75m of other placeholder lines.

 **What this means for project teams:** A change register that shows only the net figure hides most of the work your team managed. Report gross additions and reductions as well, and carry an allowance for the lines you know will arrive, such as police traffic control.

 
## Do bigger jobs and some districts change more?

 Yes to both. Larger jobs grew more and had approved additional days far more often, and districts working under the same rules finished with very different results.

 These figures use a wider set of 9,980 completed construction contracts with a recorded award amount (9,979 with cost growth), awarded from 2003 to 2026, mostly 2009 to 2025. Approved additional days are days added to the contract time by change order. Late penalties are liquidated damages, a fixed amount per day for finishing after the allowed time, and every contract carries a daily rate whether or not it was charged.

---

## Step 28: Text block 13

Paste into the post body (Rich Text).

- Median cost growth rises from -0.3% on jobs under $100k to +5.2% on jobs over $20m, and the median daily penalty rate from $570 to $2,590 a day.
- 79% of jobs over $20m had approved additional days, against 8% under $100k. On the large jobs that had them, the median extension was 112 days against a median original contract time of 580 days.
- Most jobs with approved additional days were not charged late penalties: 31% were and 69% were not. Among jobs over $20m with additional days, 23% were charged.
- The median job grew 1.9%, but total payments across all 9,979 contracts ran 5.2% above total award value, because larger jobs grow more and hold more of the dollars, and in every band a minority of jobs grew far more than the typical one.
- Across 25 districts, median cost growth runs from +5.3% in Houston to -0.5% in Lufkin, and the share of jobs charged late penalties from 13% in Odessa to 39% in San Angelo.
- Job size does not explain the district gap. Houston and Lufkin have similar typical award sizes ($1.84m and $1.62m), and on jobs of $1m to $5m alone Houston still sits at +5.7% and Lufkin at -1.1%.

 **What this means for project teams:** Use the size bands, not a flat statewide percentage, as the starting benchmark for contingency, because one figure would be too much for small jobs and too little for large ones. When you benchmark a job or a delivery team, compare jobs of similar size and type in the same region.

 
## What do environmental items cost, and what happens to them after award?

 They cost little in total, about 1.6% of construction dollars, but they change a lot after award. Erosion control is typically paid well under its bid, while hazardous work and change orders add work that was never priced as bid items.

 Environmental line items are the controls and mitigation work on a road job: erosion and sediment control, revegetation, hazardous material handling and noise walls. Texas requires a stormwater pollution prevention plan for sites that disturb an acre or more of soil under the [construction general permit](https://www.tceq.texas.gov/permitting/stormwater/construction/index) renewed from 5 March 2023, so most road jobs carry these items.

---

## Step 30: Text block 14

Paste into the post body (Rich Text).

- Environmental items appear on 81% of 9,018 completed construction contracts awarded from 2004 to 2026: erosion control on 79% of jobs, revegetation on 62%, mitigation on 5.4% and hazardous items on 1.1%.
- On the jobs that carry them they take a median 1.0% of final cost, and across all jobs combined 1.6% of construction dollars, $0.77bn of $48.9bn.
- The spread is wide. On half of those jobs the items made up between 0.2% and 2.4% of final cost, and on one job in ten more than 5.1%. Smaller jobs ran higher, at a median 1.5% on $100k to $500k jobs against 0.9% over $20m.
- Only 11% of 11,844 completed maintenance contracts carried any environmental item, so an allowance taken from a mix of construction and maintenance would understate what a construction job carries.

 
### A typical erosion-control job is paid about half its bid

---

---

## Step 32: Text block 15

Paste into the post body (Rich Text).

- The median job with erosion-control items was paid 56% of its erosion-control bid value, and one job in five (20%) was paid nothing for them.
- Adding up the dollars on all 7,074 jobs, 74% of the bid value was paid, an underrun of 26%. Revegetation underran less, with a typical 73% paid and 81% of dollars paid.
- Leaving out allowances and change order lines, 61% of erosion-control bid dollars and 74% of revegetation bid dollars were paid.
- The shortfall is too small to move whole contracts. Erosion control was paid $66.7m less than bid on those jobs, 0.17% of their $40.3bn award value, while total payments on the same contracts ran 6.3% above award.

 
### Hazardous work was paid 27 times its bid items

Paid adds those allowances and lines added after award, and each paid line belongs to one category, so the four categories do not overlap. Mitigation was paid at 1.11 times its bid value, and noise walls are its largest type.</p></div>

var nearest=function(e){var s=st.getBoundingClientRect(),x=e.clientX-s.left,y=e.clientY-s.top-HEAD,p=xs(),xc=clamp(x,p[0],p[1]),t=(xc-p[0])/Math.max(1,p[1]-p[0]),best=-1,dist=Infinity;

st.addEventListener('pointermove',function(e){if(e.pointerType==='mouse'||e.buttons)set(nearest(e));});

## Step 35: Text block 16

Paste into the post body (Rich Text).

- Hazardous items such as asbestos, lead paint and contaminated soil appear on 1.1% of construction jobs. They were bid at $1.3m in priced items and paid $35.4m, about 27 times the bid items.
- The contracts also held $7.6m of hazardous force account allowances at award, so payments were about four times everything carried for this work at award ($8.9m).
- $32.4m (92%) was paid through force account and change order lines rather than bid items: $16.4m on allowances already in the contract and $16.0m on lines added later.
- Allowances were mostly tokens. 537 of the 550 allowance lines were set at $1,000 or less, only 11 of those contracts were paid anything on them, and one $1,000 allowance was paid $4.55m.
- Contaminated soil and groundwater was the largest type at $20.9m. Five jobs account for 75% of the hazardous dollars, and the median job with hazardous items was paid $19,900, so the 27 times figure describes the 100 jobs together, not what a typical job should expect.

 Asbestos can be surveyed before tender. The US rule on [demolition and renovation](https://www.law.cornell.edu/cfr/text/40/61.145) requires a thorough inspection for asbestos before work starts, so part of that exposure can be found and priced early. Contaminated soil and groundwater are harder to see from the surface.

 
### About $129m of environmental work was paid outside the original bid items

 - 11% of construction jobs added environmental lines by change order, worth $64.2m. Of that, $35.8m re-issued an item code already in the bid, so $28.4m was for item types that had not been bid, on 8.6% of jobs.
- $37.4m was paid on force account allowances written into the contract at award but not priced by bidders, mostly the erosion control maintenance allowance ($20.0m) and the hazardous allowances ($16.4m).
- $27.9m was paid on force account and unique change order lines that had no quantity in the contract.
- Revegetation made up $40.0m of the change order lines, and noise walls and other mitigation $14.6m.

 
### Winning bidders priced environmental items in line with the estimate

## Step 37: Text block 17

Paste into the post body (Rich Text).

- Across 26,720 environmental items on 3,632 jobs, the winner's unit price was a median 1.00 times the estimate, against 1.01 for all other items. Within the same job the difference is not statistically significant (p = 0.3).
- Weighted by dollars, winners priced environmental items at 0.86 of the estimate and other items at 0.97, so the check does not rule out pricing on a few large items.
- On the median, which describes the typical line, the underrun comes from the quantities rather than from how winners priced the items.

 **What this means for project teams:** Treat 1% of final cost as a starting benchmark for environmental items, not a budget rule, because the share ranged widely between jobs. Judge where your job sits from its size, soil disturbance, landscaping scope and contamination history, and carry contamination and environmental change as named allowances rather than token sums, because priced bid items covered little of either.

 
## What does this mean for contingency and escalation?

 The Texas records point to four separate sources of movement, and each needs its own allowance. Each figure is a benchmark from Texas DOT road work, so check how closely your project resembles those jobs before you use it.

 - **Escalation.** Bid prices rose 23.4% in 2022 and 17.0% in 2023 on the Texas basket, and estimates took about a year to follow. Add escalation in the quarter prices begin to move, and check the budget against the latest bids instead of last year's.
- **Thin competition.** Jobs with one or two bidders were won a median 5% to 18% over the estimate, with a wide spread. When tender interest is thin, test the budget against that range rather than adding a fixed premium.
- **Change inside the contract.** A 5.0% net change hid 12.8% gross movement, and 78% of jobs gained an item type that was not in the tender. Start contingency from net growth on comparable jobs (a median -0.3% under $100k to +5.2% over $20m), set it above the median to match the overrun risk the project can carry, and use the gross figure to plan the change-management effort.
- **Named costs.** Police traffic control and environmental change orders recur across many jobs, and contamination is rare but large where it occurs. Carry these as named allowances so general contingency stays for risks no one has priced yet.

## Step 39: Text block 18

Paste into the post body (Rich Text).

**What this means for project teams:** Treat these Texas figures as benchmarks and check how closely your project matches the jobs behind them in size, work type, competition and timing. Set escalation from an index you can name, test the budget against the one and two bidder range when interest is thin, start contingency from net growth on comparable jobs, and report gross additions and reductions next to the net figure.

<div class="mbpefaq-acc"><div class="mbpefaq-acc-item"><h3 class="mbpefaq-acc-h"><button type="button" class="mbpefaq-acc-btn" id="mastt-bpe-faq-faqb87" aria-expanded="false" aria-controls="mastt-bpe-faq-faqp88"><span class="mbpefaq-acc-tw"><span class="mbpefaq-acc-title">How much did Texas DOT bid prices rise from 2017 to 2024?</span></span><span class="mbpefaq-acc-ico" aria-hidden="true"></span></button></h3><div class="mbpefaq-acc-panel" id="mastt-bpe-faq-faqp88" role="region" aria-labelledby="mastt-bpe-faq-faqb87"><div class="mbpefaq-acc-in"><div class="mbpefaq-acc-body"><p>A fixed basket of 523 Texas DOT bid items rose 68% between 2017 and 2024, from an index of 100 to 168.3 in winning bid prices. The engineer's estimate for the same items rose 70%, to 170.0.</p></div></div></div></div><div class="mbpefaq-acc-item"><h3 class="mbpefaq-acc-h"><button type="button" class="mbpefaq-acc-btn" id="mastt-bpe-faq-faqb89" aria-expanded="false" aria-controls="mastt-bpe-faq-faqp90"><span class="mbpefaq-acc-tw"><span class="mbpefaq-acc-title">Did winning bids come in above the engineer's estimate in 2022?</span></span><span class="mbpefaq-acc-ico" aria-hidden="true"></span></button></h3><div class="mbpefaq-acc-panel" id="mastt-bpe-faq-faqp90" role="region" aria-labelledby="mastt-bpe-faq-faqb89"><div class="mbpefaq-acc-in"><div class="mbpefaq-acc-body"><p>Yes. By 2022 the winning-bid index on the 523-item basket stood 12.7% above the estimate index for the same items, with both set to 100 in 2017, and the median state-let construction job was won 5.4% over the estimate. It was the only year from 2017 to 2024 when the median job was won above the estimate.</p></div></div></div></div><div class="mbpefaq-acc-item"><h3 class="mbpefaq-acc-h"><button type="button" class="mbpefaq-acc-btn" id="mastt-bpe-faq-faqb91" aria-expanded="false" aria-controls="mastt-bpe-faq-faqp92"><span class="mbpefaq-acc-tw"><span class="mbpefaq-acc-title">How many bidders does a Texas DOT construction job draw?</span></span><span class="mbpefaq-acc-ico" aria-hidden="true"></span></button></h3><div class="mbpefaq-acc-panel" id="mastt-bpe-faq-faqp92" role="region" aria-labelledby="mastt-bpe-faq-faqb91"><div class="mbpefaq-acc-in"><div class="mbpefaq-acc-body"><p>State-let construction jobs drew 4.7 bidders on average from 2017 to 2024. The average fell from 5.1 in 2021 to 3.9 in 2022, and single-bid jobs rose from 3.7% to 6.0% of jobs.</p></div></div></div></div><div class="mbpefaq-acc-item"><h3 class="mbpefaq-acc-h"><button type="button" class="mbpefaq-acc-btn" id="mastt-bpe-faq-faqb93" aria-expanded="false" aria-controls="mastt-bpe-faq-faqp94"><span class="mbpefaq-acc-tw"><span class="mbpefaq-acc-title">What share of bid lines are never used?</span></span><span class="mbpefaq-acc-ico" aria-hidden="true"></span></button></h3><div class="mbpefaq-acc-panel" id="mastt-bpe-faq-faqp94" role="region" aria-labelledby="mastt-bpe-faq-faqb93"><div class="mbpefaq-acc-in"><div class="mbpefaq-acc-body"><p>13.4% of bid lines, about 1 in 7, were never used on 5,265 completed Texas DOT construction contracts. Only 39.1% ended at exactly the bid quantity.</p></div></div></div></div><div class="mbpefaq-acc-item"><h3 class="mbpefaq-acc-h"><button type="button" class="mbpefaq-acc-btn" id="mastt-bpe-faq-faqb95" aria-expanded="false" aria-controls="mastt-bpe-faq-faqp96"><span class="mbpefaq-acc-tw"><span class="mbpefaq-acc-title">How much do contracts change between award and final payment?</span></span><span class="mbpefaq-acc-ico" aria-hidden="true"></span></button></h3><div class="mbpefaq-acc-panel" id="mastt-bpe-faq-faqp96" role="region" aria-labelledby="mastt-bpe-faq-faqb95"><div class="mbpefaq-acc-in"><div class="mbpefaq-acc-body"><p>Final payments ran 5.0% above award value on 5,265 completed construction contracts, but 12.8% of contract value moved across four payment categories, and 26.1% when each item is counted separately. New item types that were not in the bid added 7.5%, and 78% of jobs gained at least one.</p></div></div></div></div><div class="mbpefaq-acc-item"><h3 class="mbpefaq-acc-h"><button type="button" class="mbpefaq-acc-btn" id="mastt-bpe-faq-faqb97" aria-expanded="false" aria-controls="mastt-bpe-faq-faqp98"><span class="mbpefaq-acc-tw"><span class="mbpefaq-acc-title">How much of a Texas road job is environmental work?</span></span><span class="mbpefaq-acc-ico" aria-hidden="true"></span></button></h3><div class="mbpefaq-acc-panel" id="mastt-bpe-faq-faqp98" role="region" aria-labelledby="mastt-bpe-faq-faqb97"><div class="mbpefaq-acc-in"><div class="mbpefaq-acc-body"><p>Environmental items are on 81% of completed construction contracts and take a median 1.0% of final cost, or 1.6% of all construction dollars. Erosion control is on 79% of jobs and is typically paid 56% of its bid value.</p></div></div></div></div><div class="mbpefaq-acc-item"><h3 class="mbpefaq-acc-h"><button type="button" class="mbpefaq-acc-btn" id="mastt-bpe-faq-faqb99" aria-expanded="false" aria-controls="mastt-bpe-faq-faqp100"><span class="mbpefaq-acc-tw"><span class="mbpefaq-acc-title">How much does contamination cost compared with its bid?</span></span><span class="mbpefaq-acc-ico" aria-hidden="true"></span></button></h3><div class="mbpefaq-acc-panel" id="mastt-bpe-faq-faqp100" role="region" aria-labelledby="mastt-bpe-faq-faqb99"><div class="mbpefaq-acc-in"><div class="mbpefaq-acc-body"><p>Hazardous material items were bid at $1.3m in priced items and paid $35.4m on completed Texas DOT construction contracts, about 27 times the bid items. The contracts also held $7.6m of hazardous force account allowances at award, most set at token amounts of $1,000 or less, so payments were about four times the $8.9m carried at award. 92% of the money was paid through force account and change order lines rather than bid items, and five jobs account for 75% of it.</p></div></div></div></div></div>

var mxGen=0;
var mxZero=function(el){if(!el.hasAttribute('data-final'))el.setAttribute('data-final',el.textContent);var dec=+(el.getAttribute('data-dec')||0);el.textContent=(el.getAttribute('data-pre')||'')+(0).toFixed(dec)+(el.getAttribute('data-suf')||'');};
var mxCount=function(el,g){var to=parseFloat(el.getAttribute('data-to'));if(isNaN(to))return;var fin=el.getAttribute('data-final')||el.textContent,dec=+(el.getAttribute('data-dec')||0),pre=el.getAttribute('data-pre')||'',suf=el.getAttribute('data-suf')||'',f=new Intl.NumberFormat('en-US',{minimumFractionDigits:dec,maximumFractionDigits:dec}),t0=null,d=950;el.setAttribute('data-final',fin);var step=function(t){if(g!==mxGen)return;if(t0===null)t0=t;var p=Math.min(1,(t-t0)/d),e=1-Math.pow(1-p,3);el.textContent=pre+f.format(to*e)+suf;if(p<1)requestAnimationFrame(step);else el.textContent=fin;};requestAnimationFrame(step);};
var mxIn=function(){if(root.classList.contains('mbpefaq-in'))return;mxGen++;var g=mxGen;root.classList.add('mbpefaq-in');root.querySelectorAll('[data-to]').forEach(function(el){mxCount(el,g);});};
var mxOut=function(){if(!root.classList.contains('mbpefaq-in'))return;mxGen++;root.classList.remove('mbpefaq-in');root.querySelectorAll('[data-to]').forEach(mxZero);};
if(RM||!('IntersectionObserver' in window)){root.classList.add('mbpefaq-in');}else{root.querySelectorAll('[data-to]').forEach(mxZero);var io=new IntersectionObserver(function(es){es.forEach(function(e){if(e.isIntersecting&&e.intersectionRatio>=.15){mxIn();}else if(!e.isIntersecting){mxOut();}});},{threshold:[0,.15]});io.observe(root);}
root.querySelectorAll('.mbpefaq-tabs').forEach(function(box){var tabs=[].slice.call(box.querySelectorAll(':scope>.mbpefaq-tablist>.mbpefaq-tab')),panels=[].slice.call(box.querySelectorAll(':scope>.mbpefaq-panel'));var pick=function(v,focus){tabs.forEach(function(t){var on=t.getAttribute('data-v')===v;t.setAttribute('aria-selected',on?'true':'false');t.tabIndex=on?0:-1;if(on&&focus)t.focus();});panels.forEach(function(p){p.hidden=p.getAttribute('data-v')!==v;});box.dispatchEvent(new CustomEvent('mbpefaq-tab',{bubbles:true}));};tabs.forEach(function(t,i){t.addEventListener('click',function(){pick(t.getAttribute('data-v'));});t.addEventListener('keydown',function(e){var k=e.key,j=k==='ArrowRight'?i+1:k==='ArrowLeft'?i-1:-1;if(j<0&&k!=='ArrowLeft')return;e.preventDefault();var n=tabs[(j+tabs.length)%tabs.length];pick(n.getAttribute('data-v'),true);});});});
var mxTip=function(host){var tip=host.querySelector(':scope>.mbpefaq-tip');if(!tip){tip=document.createElement('div');tip.className='mbpefaq-tip';tip.setAttribute('aria-hidden','true');host.appendChild(tip);}return{show:function(x,y,h){tip.innerHTML=h;tip.style.left=x+'px';tip.style.top=y+'px';tip.classList.add('mbpefaq-on');},hide:function(){tip.classList.remove('mbpefaq-on');}};};
try{
root.querySelectorAll('.mbpefaq-acc').forEach(function(acc){var items=[].slice.call(acc.querySelectorAll(':scope>.mbpefaq-acc-item'));items.forEach(function(it){var b=it.querySelector('.mbpefaq-acc-btn');b.addEventListener('click',function(){var open=!it.classList.contains('mbpefaq-open');items.forEach(function(o){o.classList.remove('mbpefaq-open');o.querySelector('.mbpefaq-acc-btn').setAttribute('aria-expanded','false');});if(open){it.classList.add('mbpefaq-open');b.setAttribute('aria-expanded','true');}});});});
}catch(e){}
})();</script>
</div>

## Step 41: Text block 19

## Sources and methodology

 **Records.** Our own figures come from an internal Mastt study of public Texas DOT contract records:

 - The price index starts from the unit prices on about 13,700 lettings from 2017 to 2024.
- It holds a fixed basket of 523 items priced per unit, each with at least 15 winning-bid prices in every year, which together appear on about 10,800 lettings.
- It combines each item's yearly median winning and estimated price using fixed weights: each item's winning-bid dollars for the bid index and estimate dollars for the estimate index, over 2017 to 2024 combined.
- The letting comparisons use 5,912 state-let construction lettings, with the low bid compared against the engineer's estimate, and the all-lettings view adds maintenance and local lettings.
- The price-history model prices each 2023 and 2024 letting from the median winning unit price of every item over the previous 12 months. The miss is the gap between a predicted total and the winning bid, counted the same whether over or under, as a percentage of the winning bid, and the median miss is the middle of those gaps.

 **Post-award figures.** These link each bid line to the quantity finally paid. They cover:

 - 341,637 bid lines on 5,265 completed construction contracts let from 2015 to 2024.
- The same 5,265 contracts, with $30.1bn awarded, for the gross and net change.
- 9,980 completed contracts with a recorded award (9,979 with cost growth), awarded from 2003 to 2026, mostly 2009 to 2025, for job size and district.
- 9,018 completed construction contracts awarded from 2004 to 2026, for environmental items, built from more than 80,000 paid environmental lines, with 11,844 maintenance contracts for comparison.

 Environmental lines are tagged from item numbers and hand-reviewed descriptions, and each line belongs to one category and one type. Dollars are nominal.

 **Limits.** These are signals, not proof:

 - Texas figures describe Texas DOT work only.
- Recent contracts lean toward shorter jobs because long jobs are not final yet.
- Environmental work paid inside other items is not visible, and change orders classified from free text give a lower bound.
- National index values are annual averages of the published quarterly or monthly series, set to 100 in 2017.
- The Mastt model result is a test of a simple price-history approach on 2023 and 2024 lettings and does not describe any commercial estimating tool.

 Includes public sector information from Australian federal, state, territory and local governments, used under Creative Commons Attribution 4.0 licences.
